间充质干细胞治疗HPV驱动的宫颈癌的免疫学后果:系统性综述与分层框架

Immunological consequences of mesenchymal stem cell therapy in HPV-driven cervical cancer: a systematic review and stratification framework.
作者： Hafsa Iqbal, Waad H Abuwatfa
摘要中文翻译每年有超过 660,000 名女性患有 宫颈癌,这是由高危 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 持续感染引起的。已发表的间充质干细胞/基质细胞(MSC)作为免疫调节剂的研究是相互矛盾的,一些报告抑制肿瘤,另一些报告促进肿瘤。这些悖论的机制尚未得到系统研究。本综述旨在评估 HPV 相关 宫颈癌 中 MSC 介导的免疫逃避,量化文献中的 HPV 分层失败,并制定将 MSC 行为与 HPV 致癌活性联系起来的预测分层模型。根据 PRISMA 2020 指南对 8 个数据库进行了系统评价。如果研究报告 MSC 干预 宫颈癌 并产生一种或多种免疫相关结果,则研究被纳入。HPV 分层差距分析与数据提取和偏倚风险评估一起进行。纳入 14 项研究:13 项已完成的临床前研究和 1 项正在进行的临床试验。MSC 的作用包括通过腺苷、IL-10 和 M2 巨噬细胞极化进行免疫逃避,以及通过 STAT3、Wnt/β-Catenin 和表观遗传途径抑制肿瘤。在 13 项已完成的研究中,没有一项研究将 HPV 致癌活性作为实验变量进行测量。只有两个包含 HPV 阴性 队列研究。记录了 MSC 外泌体对 HPV16 和 HPV18 细胞系的不同影响,但与 HPV 基因型无关。HPV 致癌活性是 宫颈癌 中 MSC 活性的未测量且合理的修饰因子。按 HPV 致癌状态进行分层应该是可解释和可翻译证据的方法学要求,因为 E6/E7 信号传导与 PDL-1、TGF-β、STAT-3 和 NF-κB 相关,所有这些都与 MSC 免疫调节途径相关。建议采用预测分层系统和最低报告标准。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
每年有超过 660,000 名女性患有 宫颈癌,这是由高危 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 持续感染引起的。本综述旨在评估 HPV 相关 宫颈癌 中 MSC 介导的免疫逃避,量化文献中的 HPV 分层失败,并制定将 MSC 行为与 HPV 致癌活性联系起来的预测分层模型。
证据基础与方法
根据 PRISMA 2020 指南对 8 个数据库进行了系统评价。已发表的间充质干细胞/基质细胞(MSC)作为免疫调节剂的研究是相互矛盾的,一些报告抑制肿瘤,另一些报告促进肿瘤。如果研究报告 MSC 干预 宫颈癌 并产生一种或多种免疫相关结果,则研究被纳入。HPV 分层差距分析与数据提取和偏倚风险评估一起进行。
共识与结论
MSC 的作用包括通过腺苷、IL-10 和 M2 巨噬细胞极化进行免疫逃避,以及通过 STAT3、Wnt/β-Catenin 和表观遗传途径抑制肿瘤。HPV 致癌活性是 宫颈癌 中 MSC 活性的未测量且合理的修饰因子。建议采用预测分层系统和最低报告标准。
争议与缺口
这些悖论的机制尚未得到系统研究。在 13 项已完成的研究中,没有一项研究将 HPV 致癌活性作为实验变量进行测量。记录了 MSC 外泌体对 HPV16 和 HPV18 细胞系的不同影响,但与 HPV 基因型无关。
科研与实践启示
按 HPV 致癌状态进行分层应该是可解释和可翻译证据的方法学要求,因为 E6/E7 信号传导与 PDL-1、TGF-β、STAT-3 和 NF-κB 相关,所有这些都与 MSC 免疫调节途径相关。

Immunological consequences of mesenchymal stem cell therapy in HPV-driven cervical cancer: a systematic review and stratification framework.

Authors: Hafsa Iqbal, Waad H Abuwatfa
Original abstract (English)
Over 660,000 women develop cervical cancer annually, caused by persistent infection with high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV). Published studies of mesenchymal stem/stromal cells (MSCs) as immunomodulatory agents are contradictory, with some reporting tumor suppression and others tumor promotion. The mechanism of these paradoxes has not been systematically investigated. This review aims to assess MSC-mediated immune evasion in HPV-related cervical cancer, quantify HPV stratification failure in the literature, and formulate a predictive stratification model linking MSC behavior to HPV oncogenic activity. A systematic review was performed following PRISMA 2020 guidelines across 8 databases. Studies were included if they reported an MSC intervention for cervical cancer with one or more immune-related outcomes. HPV stratification gap analysis was performed alongside data extraction and risk-of-bias evaluation. 14 studies were included: 13 completed preclinical and 1 ongoing clinical trial. MSC effects included immune evasion through adenosine, IL-10, and M2 macrophage polarization, and tumor suppression through STAT3, Wnt/β-Catenin, and epigenetic pathways. Of 13 completed studies, none measured HPV oncogenic activity as an experimental variable. Only two included an HPV-negative cohort. Differential effects of MSC exosomes on HPV16 and HPV18 cell lines were recorded but not linked to HPV genotype. HPV oncogenic activity is an unmeasured and plausible modifier of MSC activity in cervical cancer. Stratification by HPV oncogenic status should be a methodological requirement for interpretable and translatable evidence, because E6/E7 signalling is linked to PDL-1, TGF-β, STAT-3 and NF-κB, all linked to MSC immunomodulatory pathways. A predictive stratification system and minimum reporting standards are suggested.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Over 660,000 women develop cervical cancer annually, caused by persistent infection with high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV). Consensus and conclusions

HPV oncogenic activity is an unmeasured and plausible modifier of MSC activity in cervical cancer. A predictive stratification system and minimum reporting standards are suggested.

处于衰老和癌症十字路口的肿瘤病毒:分子机制和关键信号通路。

Oncoviruses at the Crossroads of Ageing and Cancer: Molecular Mechanisms and Key Signalling Pathways.
作者： Noor Allehyani, Mohammed Alissa, Abdullah Alghamdi, Mohammed A Alshehri, Ghadah S Abusalim, Tawfiq N Juraybi, Meshari A Alsuwat, Awaji Y Safhi
摘要中文翻译肿瘤病毒是导致大部分人类癌症的原因,并且已成为 宿主 细胞途径的关键调节剂,该途径也控制着衰老。病毒肿瘤发生、衰老和癌症的融合反映了共同的分子机制,包括基因组不稳定性、表观遗传改变、端粒磨损和慢性炎症。越来越多的证据表明,人乳头瘤病毒、Epstein-Barr病毒、乙型和丙型肝炎病毒以及卡波西肉瘤相关疱疹病毒等肿瘤病毒会主动重编程宿主信号网络,以促进持续感染、免疫逃避和恶性转化。这篇综述综合了目前关于肿瘤病毒如何与衰老和癌症的关键特征相交叉的知识,特别强调了桥接这些过程的信号通路。包括 p53、mTOR、NF-κB、PI3K/AKT 和 Wnt/β-catenin 在内的中央通路经常成为病毒蛋白的目标,导致细胞存活、增殖和衰老失调。这些相互作用不仅促进肿瘤发生,而且加速与衰老相关的细胞功能障碍,例如免疫衰老和炎症。此外,我们讨论了病毒诱导的肿瘤微环境调节和 宿主 免疫应答 如何促进衰老与癌症之间的动态相互作用。了解这些相互关联的途径可以为了解肿瘤病毒作为与年龄相关的衰退和癌症发展的驱动因素的双重作用提供有价值的见解。阐明这些机制可能会揭示预防和治疗老龄化人群中与病毒相关的恶性肿瘤的新治疗靶点和策略。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
这篇综述探讨了 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 与衰老和癌症关键标志相交叉的分子机制,重点关注桥接这些过程的信号通路。核心问题是 HPV 如何调节 宿主 细胞通路以促进持续感染、免疫逃避和恶性转化,重点是其在与年龄相关的细胞功能障碍中的作用。
证据基础与方法
该综述是对 HPV 在衰老和癌症中作用的当前知识的叙述性综合。它不报告特定的数据库、搜索日期或荟萃分析方法。这些证据源自对 HPV 及其分子相互作用的现有文献的一般科学理解和解释。
共识与结论
人们一致认为,包括 HPV 在内的肿瘤病毒会主动重编程 宿主 信号网络,以促进持续感染、免疫逃避和恶性转化。p53、mTOR、NF-κB、PI3K/AKT 和 Wnt/β-连环蛋白等中央通路经常成为病毒蛋白的目标,导致细胞存活、增殖和衰老失调。这些相互作用导致肿瘤发生和衰老相关的细胞功能障碍。
争议与缺口
该综述强调,病毒肿瘤发生、衰老和癌症的趋同反映了共同的分子机制,但证据并未报告具体的争议或相互矛盾的发现。对于病毒诱导的肿瘤微环境调节的精确机制和宿主 免疫应答缺乏详细的讨论。
科研与实践启示
了解这些相互关联的途径可能会揭示预防和治疗老龄化人群中与病毒相关的恶性肿瘤的新治疗靶点和策略。该综述表明,需要进一步研究来探索病毒诱导的肿瘤微环境调节与 宿主 免疫应答 之间的动态相互作用。

Oncoviruses at the Crossroads of Ageing and Cancer: Molecular Mechanisms and Key Signalling Pathways.

Authors: Noor Allehyani, Mohammed Alissa, Abdullah Alghamdi, Mohammed A Alshehri, Ghadah S Abusalim, Tawfiq N Juraybi, Meshari A Alsuwat, Awaji Y Safhi
Original abstract (English)
Oncoviruses are responsible for a substantial fraction of human cancers and have emerged as critical modulators of host cellular pathways that also govern ageing. The convergence of viral oncogenesis, ageing, and cancer reflects shared molecular mechanisms, including genomic instability, epigenetic alterations, telomere attrition, and chronic inflammation. Increasing evidence indicates that oncoviruses such as human papillomavirus, Epstein-Barr virus, hepatitis B and C viruses, and Kaposi's sarcoma-associated herpesvirus actively reprogramme host signalling networks to promote persistent infection, immune evasion, and malignant transformation. This review synthesises current knowledge on how oncoviruses intersect with key hallmarks of ageing and cancer, with particular emphasis on the signalling pathways that bridge these processes. Central pathways, including p53, mTOR, NF-κB, PI3K/AKT, and Wnt/β-catenin, are frequently targeted by viral proteins, leading to dysregulated cell survival, proliferation, and senescence. These interactions not only facilitate tumourigenesis but also accelerate ageing-associated cellular dysfunctions, such as immunosenescence and inflammaging. Furthermore, we discuss how virus-induced modulation of the tumour microenvironment and host immune responses contributes to the dynamic interplay between ageing and cancer. Understanding these interconnected pathways provides valuable insights into the dual role of oncoviruses as drivers of both age-related decline and cancer development. Elucidating these mechanisms may reveal novel therapeutic targets and strategies for preventing and treating virus-associated malignancies in ageing populations.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
This review explores the molecular mechanisms by which human papillomaviruses (HPV) intersect with key hallmarks of ageing and cancer, focusing on the signalling pathways that bridge these processes. The central question is how HPV modulates host cellular pathways to promote persistent infection, immune evasion, and malignant transformation, with an emphasis on its role in age-related cellular dysfunctions.
Evidence base and method
The review is a narrative synthesis of current knowledge on HPV's role in ageing and cancer. It does not report specific databases, search dates, or meta-analysis methods. The evidence is derived from general scientific understanding and interpretation of existing literature on HPV and its molecular interactions.
Consensus and conclusions
There is consensus that oncoviruses, including HPV, actively reprogramme host signalling networks to promote persistent infection, immune evasion, and malignant transformation. Central pathways such as p53, mTOR, NF-κB, PI3K/AKT, and Wnt/β-catenin are frequently targeted by viral proteins, leading to dysregulated cell survival, proliferation, and senescence. These interactions contribute to both tumourigenesis and ageing-associated cellular dysfunctions.
Controversies and gaps
The review highlights that the convergence of viral oncogenesis, ageing, and cancer reflects shared molecular mechanisms, but the evidence does not report specific controversies or conflicting findings. There is a lack of detailed discussion on the precise mechanisms of virus-induced modulation of the tumour microenvironment and host immune responses.
Research and practice implications
Understanding these interconnected pathways may reveal novel therapeutic targets and strategies for preventing and treating virus-associated malignancies in ageing populations. The review suggests that further research is needed to explore the dynamic interplay between virus-induced modulation of the tumour microenvironment and host immune responses.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

研究提供更多证据表明 HPV 疫苗 对男性有益

Studies provide more evidence that HPV vaccines benefit men
新闻简报近期研究为人乳头瘤病毒疫苗(HPV疫苗)对男性的益处提供了更多证据,显示其可显著降低肛门和生殖器HPV感染风险。一项日本随机试验发现,九价HPV疫苗(Gardasil 9)可预防92%的常见HPV持续性感染,以及73%的与癌症相关HPV感染。加拿大一项真实世界研究证实,23岁前接种疫苗的男性罹患肛门HPV感染的风险降低60%,而晚期接种者则无降低效果。HPV疫苗还可将阴茎和口腔感染风险分别降低51%至100%。这些发现支持采用HPV疫苗性别中和策略的建议,因早期接种效果最佳。然而,全球覆盖率仍存在缺口,东地中海和东南亚等地区接种受限。研究强调应在性活动开始前接种疫苗,因HPV相关癌症可能在数十年后发展。未来研究,包括预计于2029年启动的一项中国试验,可能进一步确认疫苗对肛门癌、阴茎癌和口腔癌等癌症的保护效果。
查看新闻五要素
时间
相关研究于2026年发表,其中日本试验于本月早些时候发表,加拿大研究于昨日发表。随机试验的中位随访时间为2.4年。
地点与对象
这些研究涉及日本和加拿大的男性,日本的试验主要针对 16 至 26 岁的男性,加拿大的研究则针对 16 至 23 岁的同性恋和双性恋男性。系统评价包括 2007 年至 2024 年的 21 项研究。
事件
三项新研究表明,HPV 疫苗 可显着降低男性肛门和生殖器 HPV 感染的风险。日本的一项随机试验发现 Gardasil 9 降低了肛门或生殖器 HPV 感染的风险,加拿大的一项真实世界研究证实了 疫苗接种 对年轻男性的有效性。
规模、影响与风险
疫苗 可以预防 92% 的最常见 HPV 病毒株持续性感染,以及 73% 的由不太常见但与癌症相关的病毒株引起的感染。它还显示阴茎和口腔 HPV 感染的风险分别降低了 51% 至 100%。
应对、状态与不确定性
研究正在进行中,日本的试验对参与者进行了 2.4 年的跟踪。研究人员指出,未来的研究可能会证实对 HPV 相关癌症的保护作用。世界卫生组织估计男孩的覆盖率差距,特别是在某些地区。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Studies provide more evidence that HPV vaccines benefit men

News Brief
Recent studies provide more evidence that HPV vaccines benefit men by significantly reducing the risk of anal and genital HPV infections. A Japanese randomized trial found Gardasil 9 prevented 92% of persistent infections with common HPV strains and 73% of those linked to cancer. A Canadian real-world study confirmed that men vaccinated by age 23 had a 60% lower risk of anal HPV infections, while those vaccinated later showed no reduction. The vaccine also demonstrated a 51% to 100% risk reduction for penile and oral infections, respectively. These findings support the recommendation for gender-neutral HPV vaccination strategies, as early vaccination is most effective. However, coverage gaps persist globally, with limited access in regions like the Eastern Mediterranean and Southeast Asia. The studies highlight the importance of vaccinating before sexual activity begins, as HPV-related cancers can develop over decades. Future research, including a Chinese trial expected by 2029, may further confirm these protective effects against cancers such as anal, penile, and oral cancers.

Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search CLOSE Search Support Menu Studies provide more evidence that HPV vaccines benefit men Liz Szabo, MA Human Papillomavirus (HPV) Drs Producoes/iStock Share Copied to clipboard Three new studies add to a growing body of research showing that men and boys vaccinated against the human papillomavirus (HPV) are much less likely than others to be infected with the ubiquitous virus, which can cause cancers of the head and neck, anus, and penis, as well as genital warts. A randomized trial from Japan, published in The Lancet Infectious Diseases earlier this month, found that Gardasil 9—which protects against nine HPV strains—dramatically reduced the risk of developing anal or genital HPV infections. The vaccine prevented 92% of persistent infections with the most common strains of HPV and 73% caused by strains that are less common but still able to cause cancer. The researchers followed more than 1,000 men for a median of 2.4 years. A real-world study from Canada, published in The Journal of Infectious Diseases yesterday, focused on gay and bisexual men ages 16 to 23. The study of nearly 1,200 men confirmed that HPV vaccination is most effective when given at a young age, before people are exposed to the virus. Men vaccinated by age 23 were 60% less likely to have anal HPV infections than others in the study. The researchers found no reduction in HPV risk among men vaccinated at age 27 or older. A review of 21 studies performed from 2007 to 2024, published in The International Journal of Infectious Diseases last week, found that HPV vaccinations prevent anal, penile, and oral infections in men. Studies included Gardail 9, as well as an earlier vaccine that prevented four HPV strains. While results varied from study to study, researchers from University of Florida and Merck Research Laboratories found that evidence suggests that vaccines provide good protection, ranging from a 51% reduction in the risk of penile HPV infections to a 100% reduction in the risk of oral HPV infections. Merck is the manufacturer of Gardasil 9. “It’s exciting,” Neil Gross, MD, director of clinical research in the department of head and neck surgery at MD Anderson Cancer Center, told CIDRAP News when asked about the new findings. “We’ve known vaccination is the right thing to do,” said Gross, who was not involved in the studies. “That’s why HPV is recommended as a gender-neutral vaccination strategy. It’s nice to see some data quantifying the benefit.” Good news for men and public health HPV affects about 80% of sexually active women and more than 90% of men. A small number of people develop persistent HPV infections, which can lead to cancer. HPV spreads through intimate contact, including vaginal, anal, and oral sex, and even deep kissing. The randomized, controlled trial from Japan “demonstrates that there is real-world, personal benefit to HPV vaccination for boys and men,” said Demetre Daskalakis, MD, MPH, chief medical officer at the Callen-Lorde Community Health Center in New York, which provides LGBTQ health care. “Vaccination prevented persistent infections which can be associated with cancers... regardless of the gender of sexual partners,” said Daskalakis, former director of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s National Center for Immunization and Respiratory Diseases. The systematic review also “re-affirms the observation that the HPV vaccine is good for public health by interrupting the chain of sexual transmission,” said Daskalakis, who was not involved in the new research. The studies illustrate why it’s important to vaccinate people before they are sexually active, Daskalakis said. The American Academy of Pediatrics recommends boys and girls begin the HPV vaccine series at ages 9 to 12 years. “Planning for HPV cancer prevention is like packing an umbrella before the rain starts,” Daskalakis said. “We know that most people will eventually become sexually active, so it's smartest to prepare before the exposure happens.” Evidence to change vaccine policy In the United States, the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approved HPV shots for girls in 2007 and for boys in 2011. About 78% of teens have received one or more doses of the vaccine, although only 63% have received all recommended doses. Children receive two to three doses of the HPV vaccine, depending on the age at which they’re first vaccinated. Planning for HPV cancer prevention is like packing an umbrella before the rain starts. At least 85 countries now offer HPV vaccines to both boys and girls. But there are huge gaps in coverage. The World Health Organization (WHO) estimates that none of the boys in the Eastern Mediterranean and Southeast Asia have completed the three-dose HPV vaccine regimen, while only 1% of boys have in the African and Pacific regions. Although Japan approved HPV shots for boys in 2020, the vaccines are not yet part of the country’s recommended and subsidized routine vaccination program. Taito Kitano, DrPH, a researcher at Nara Prefecture General Medical Center in Nara, Japan, described the new studies as “important evidence indicating the beneficial impacts of HPV vaccination in males.” Kitano, who also was not involved in the new studies, said, “these data explain why we are recommending HPV vaccination in boys, as well.” In a review published in April, Kitano and his colleagues found that vaccination was linked to a nearly 50% drop in the risk of any HPV-related cancer in men. Because HPV-related tumors can take decades to develop, there are relatively few studies showing changes in cancer or even pre-cancer rates. Given the results of studies like those published this year, many experts predict that future studies will also find that HPV vaccines protect men from anal, penile, and oral cancers. Although research shows that HPV vaccination prevents cervical cancer, there is much less evidence about other cancer types. Studying cervical cancer in vaccinated women is easier, because those cancers often develop when women are in their 30s. Head and neck cancers tend to be diagnosed in men in their 60s. “Vaccination programs started in girls and were implemented later in boys,” wrote Miyuki Sawata, PhD, and Alain Luxembourg, MD, PhD, two of the authors of the randomized trial of Gardasil 9 in Japan, in an email. “It is expected that it will take a few more years to observe a decrease in non-cervical cancers related to HPV in vaccinated populations.” A randomized trial in China is expected to help answer these questions by 2029. The study will compare rates of anogenital cancers and precancers in vaccinated and unvaccinated men. Another ongoing international trial, scheduled to conclude in 2028, will measure rates of persistent HPV-related oral infections in men vaccinated with Gardasil 9 and in unvaccinated men. A very common virus Preventing head and neck cancers is particularly important, because there are no proven ways to screen for these tumors, which can develop deep in the throat or on the back of the tongue, Gross said. Many head and neck cancers are found only after becoming advanced, when harsh treatments are necessary. “We don’t have a Pap smear for the throat,” Gross said. People with head and neck cancers experience “a big impact on quality of life and function, and the ability to participate in society the way they did before,” he said. Surveys show that relatively few people have heard of HPV. While more people know that HPV causes cervical cancer, more than 70% are unaware that HPV causes head and neck cancers, now the most common types of cancer caused by HPV. “I can't believe that it's not better known that HPV is associated with head and neck cancers,” said Kevin Conlon, who was diagnosed with HPV-related cancer four years ago, after finding a lump in his neck while shaving. “If the vaccine had been available earlier, I would have encouraged my son to take it.” We don’t have a Pap smear for the throat. Conlon encourages people to vaccinate both boys and girls against HPV. “I hear some of the concerns by some who don't want their children to be vaccinated,” said Conlon, now 65. “Some are just anti-vaxx on principle, and others worry that having the vaccine will somehow give their children tacit permission to have sex.” People “need to know that a vaccine is a lot cheaper and a hell of a lot less painful than surgery, chemo, and radiation.” Related news Previous Next People vaccinated against HPV have lower risk of head and neck cancer, study suggests Liz Szabo Analysis: Cervical cancer screenings vary widely among federally funded health centers Sarah Boden Women often opt for self-collection HPV screening, new studies show Meghan Holohan Slight gains made in global childhood vaccination rates, says new report Meghan Holohan HIV dramatically increases the risk of HPV-related anal cancer, data show Liz Szabo Some patients opt for at-home cervical screening for HPV, but more outreach is needed, study finds Meghan Holohan HPV vaccination eliminated cervical cancer deaths in young UK women in recent years, study suggests Meghan Holohan ‘Everyone who has a cervix is at risk’: Transgender people often miss out on cervical cancer screenings Liz Szabo This week's top reads Will flu, COVID-19 vaccines be available this fall? Time
The studies were published in 2026, with the Japanese trial published earlier this month and the Canadian study published yesterday. The randomized trial was followed for a median of 2.4 years.
Location and population
The studies involved men in Japan and Canada, with the Japanese trial focusing on men aged 16 to 26 and the Canadian study on gay and bisexual men aged 16 to 23. The systematic review included 21 studies from 2007 to 2024.
Event
Three new studies show that HPV vaccines significantly reduce the risk of anal and genital HPV infections in men. A randomized trial from Japan found Gardasil 9 reduced the risk of developing anal or genital HPV infections, and a real-world study from Canada confirmed the effectiveness of vaccination in younger men.
Scale, impact and risk
The vaccine prevented 92% of persistent infections with the most common HPV strains and 73% of infections caused by less common but cancer-associated strains. It also showed a 51% to 100% reduction in the risk of penile and oral HPV infections, respectively.
Response, status and uncertainty
The studies are ongoing, with the Japanese trial following participants for 2.4 years. The researchers noted that future studies will likely confirm the protective effects against HPV-related cancers. The WHO estimates coverage gaps in boys, particularly in certain regions.

结合非编码RNA谱系与HPV基因分型提升宫颈癌风险评估 - Bioengineer.org

Combining noncoding RNA profiles and HPV genotyping improves cervical cancer risk assessment - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报《分子生物学报告》近期的一篇综述提出了一种新颖的筛查理念,将高危型HPV基因分型数据与宿主非编码RNA特征谱系相结合,以更好地预测哪些感染可能进展为宫颈癌。HPV基因分型可识别病毒类型,而微小RNA、长链非编码RNA及环状RNA等非编码RNA则可揭示病毒是否正在主动改变细胞通路。作者认为,这种双重策略有望将一过性低风险感染与持续性高风险病例区分开来,从而可能减少不必要的随访。然而,他们强调该方法尚未获得临床验证;现有证据主要来自细胞系研究和小规模回顾性研究。需要前瞻性大规模临床试验来确认其诊断价值并建立标准化阈值,方可考虑常规应用。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于2026年8月28日;该综述于当日发布(证据N1)。
地点与对象
文章针对感染高危型HPV的宿主,特别是接受宫颈癌筛查的女性;文中未报告具体地理位置(证据N2、N3、N4)。
事件
《分子生物学报告》的一篇综述提出将高危型HPV基因分型与非编码RNA谱系分析相结合,以区分一过性感染与潜在致癌感染。该提案被呈现为研究理念而非已验证的临床检测方法,作者指出存在显著的证据缺口(证据N1、N3、N40、N43)。
规模、影响与风险
大多数HPV感染为一过性且可被清除而无害,而少数感染可持续存在并为癌前病变及侵袭性宫颈癌的发生创造条件。高危型HPV感染较为常见,但宫颈癌在感染者中仍相对少见(证据N4、N5、N6)。
应对、状态与不确定性
该策略尚未获得验证;综述强调需开展前瞻性研究以建立阈值、重现性及临床实用性。当前状态为探索性,其向常规实践转化的不确定性仍存(证据N40、N43、N44)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Combining noncoding RNA profiles and HPV genotyping improves cervical cancer risk assessment - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A recent review in Molecular Biology Reports outlines a novel screening concept that merges HPV genotype data with host non‐coding RNA signatures to better predict which infections may progress toward cervical cancer. While HPV genotyping identifies the viral type, non‐coding RNAs such as microRNAs, long non‐coding RNAs, and circular RNAs could reveal whether the virus is actively altering cellular pathways. The authors argue this dual approach could separate transient, low‐risk infections from persistent, high‐risk cases, potentially reducing unnecessary follow‐ups. However, they stress that the method has not yet been clinically validated; existing evidence comes mainly from cell‐line work and small retrospective studies. Prospective, large‐scale trials are needed to confirm its diagnostic value and establish standardized thresholds before routine implementation.

HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, August 29, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Biology Combining noncoding RNA profiles and HPV genotyping improves cervical cancer risk assessment by Bioengineer August 28, 2026 in Biology Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A new molecular screening strategy could help doctors distinguish human papillomavirus infections that are likely to disappear from those that may be moving toward cervical cancer, according to a review published in Molecular Biology Reports. The approach combines HPV genotyping with measurements of non-coding RNAs—molecules that do not produce proteins but can exert powerful control over gene activity. By reading both the virus and the host cell, researchers argue, screening could move beyond the simple question of whether high-risk HPV is present and begin to estimate whether an infection is biologically active, persistent and potentially dangerous. The distinction matters because infection with a high-risk HPV type is common, while cervical cancer is relatively uncommon among infected individuals. Most HPV infections are transient and are cleared by the immune system without causing lasting cellular damage. A smaller proportion persist, and persistent infection can create the conditions for precancerous lesions and, eventually, invasive cancer. Current screening methods—including HPV DNA testing, cytology and visual examination—have improved early detection, but they do not always identify which positive results represent a short-lived infection and which signal a transformation process already underway. This uncertainty can lead to repeated testing, unnecessary procedures or delayed attention to genuinely high-risk disease. HPV genotyping supplies an important part of the missing information. Rather than reporting only that HPV is present, genotyping identifies the specific viral type or group. Some strains, particularly HPV16 and HPV18, are strongly associated with cervical cancer, while other high-risk types carry different levels of risk. Genotyping is therefore useful for surveillance, triage and evaluating vaccination programs. Yet the viral type alone cannot reveal whether the virus is actively altering the host cell. Two people infected with the same high-risk strain may have very different outcomes, depending on viral persistence, immune responses, viral gene expression and changes accumulating in cervical cells. Discover more cancer Cancer Screening Information Biotechnology News Feed Bioengineering Career Resources Bioengineering News Subscription The proposed second layer of testing focuses on non-coding RNAs, or ncRNAs. These include microRNAs, long non-coding RNAs and circular RNAs, each with distinct molecular properties and functions. MicroRNAs are short RNA molecules that bind to messenger RNAs and usually reduce the production of specific proteins. Long non-coding RNAs can influence gene transcription, organize protein complexes or alter the stability and availability of other RNAs. Circular RNAs form closed loops that can be unusually resistant to degradation and may regulate gene expression by binding microRNAs or interacting with proteins. Together, these molecules create a regulatory network that can reflect how a cervical cell is responding to HPV. The biological connection between HPV and ncRNAs is central to the review’s argument. High-risk HPV produces oncoproteins, particularly E6 and E7, that interfere with cellular safeguards governing genome stability, cell division and programmed cell death. E6 can promote the degradation or functional suppression of the tumor-suppressor protein p53, while E7 disrupts the retinoblastoma pathway, which normally restrains inappropriate entry into the cell cycle. These disturbances can reshape the cell’s transcriptional and post-transcriptional programs. In turn, altered ncRNAs may help sustain proliferation, weaken apoptosis, modify immune signaling and promote invasion—features associated with malignant progression. Several candidate molecules illustrate how such signals might be used. The review discusses microRNAs whose abundance changes in HPV-associated cervical disease, including miR-21, miR-155, miR-375, miR-145 and miR-106b-5p. Some have been linked with enhanced growth, migration or poor prognosis, while others appear to restrain invasion or proliferation and become reduced during disease progression. The molecular effects are not interchangeable: miR-21, for example, has been associated with pathways affecting growth and migration, whereas reduced miR-375 has been linked to loss of control over transcription factor SP1. These patterns could form part of a multi-marker signature rather than serve as stand-alone diagnostic tests. Long non-coding and circular RNAs may add information about the architecture and persistence of the cancer-associated regulatory network. HOTAIR has been associated with cervical cancer progression and has been studied in serum and vaginal discharge, while MALAT1 can be regulated by the HPV16 E7 protein and has been connected to cancer progression, metastasis, immunity and treatment resistance. Other studies summarized in the review describe HPV-related changes in lnc-FANCI-2 and circular RNAs involved in regulatory axes such as miR-1236-3p/TRIM37 and miR-1179/ABL2. Because circular RNAs are structurally stable and many ncRNAs can circulate in blood or other biological fluids, they are attractive candidates for liquid biopsy tests that might avoid or complement tissue sampling. Discover more Colleges & Universities Printing & Publishing Scientific Bioengineering Video Library Science In practice, an integrated test could combine several forms of evidence from one sample. HPV DNA analysis would identify the viral genotype, while RNA profiling could measure a panel of host-response molecules and perhaps indicators of active oncogenic signaling. A computational model could then classify a patient’s result into a lower- or higher-risk category, guiding the interval for repeat screening or the need for colposcopy and biopsy. The same principle could potentially be adapted to self-collected vaginal samples, cervical specimens or blood-based assays, although the review does not present a clinically validated test or a new patient cohort. It describes a diagnostic framework whose usefulness depends on selecting robust markers, standardizing laboratory methods and proving that the combined signal predicts outcomes better than existing screening. That evidence gap is the most important qualification. The authors emphasize that individual ncRNAs have shown promising associations with HPV-mediated carcinogenesis, but evidence for their combined clinical use with HPV genotyping remains limited. Biomarker levels can vary with age, inflammation, hormonal status, sampling technique, disease stage and the composition of the surrounding tissue. Results generated in cell lines or small retrospective studies may not translate directly to diverse populations or routine clinics. Prospective studies will need to follow HPV-positive individuals over time, compare transient and persistent infections, test different viral genotypes and establish thresholds that are reproducible across laboratories. The review also notes that no datasets were generated or analyzed for the study itself, because it is a narrative synthesis of existing evidence. If validated, the strategy could make cervical screening more precise without replacing established prevention tools. HPV vaccination remains fundamental, and conventional HPV testing and cytology remain valuable for identifying people who need further assessment. The promise of ncRNA profiling is to add biological context: not only which virus is present, but how strongly it is influencing the host cell. Such information could reduce the burden of unnecessary follow-up while directing clinical attention toward infections with molecular signs of transformation. For now, the concept is best viewed as an emerging precision-screening platform rather than a ready-to-use diagnostic. Its viral-news appeal lies in a simple idea with complex biology: the future of cervical cancer detection may depend on listening simultaneously to the genome of the virus and the regulatory RNA language of the cell it infects. Time
Published on August 28, 2026; the review was released on that date (evidence N1).
Location and population
The article addresses humans infected with high‐risk human papillomavirus, especially women undergoing cervical cancer screening; no specific geographic location is reported (evidence N2, N3, N4).
Event
A review in Molecular Biology Reports proposes combining HPV genotyping with non‐coding RNA profiling to distinguish transient from potentially oncogenic infections. The proposal is presented as a research concept, not a validated clinical test, and the authors note significant evidence gaps (evidence N1, N3, N40, N43).
Scale, impact and risk
Most HPV infections are transient and cleared without harm, while a smaller proportion persist and can create conditions for precancerous lesions and invasive cervical cancer. High‐risk HPV infection is common, but cervical cancer remains relatively uncommon among infected individuals (evidence N4, N5, N6).
Response, status and uncertainty
The strategy remains unvalidated; the review highlights the need for prospective studies to establish thresholds, reproducibility, and clinical utility. Current status is exploratory, with uncertainty about translation to routine practice (evidence N40, N43, N44).

研究检查干细胞移植后 HPV 抗体的耐久性和 免疫应答 - Bioengineer.org

Study examines HPV antibody durability and immune response after stem cell transplantation - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报一项在泰国开展的先导研究调查了异基因造血干细胞移植(HSCT)后青少年接种人乳头瘤病毒(HPV)疫苗疫苗的抗体应答及免疫持久性。研究发现,针对HPV16和HPV18的四价HPV 疫苗疫苗接种两剂后,大多数参与者产生了强烈且持久的抗体应答。首剂接种后6个月,54.5%的参与者对HPV16血清阳转,68.2%对HPV18血清阳转;第二剂接种后,上述比例分别升至95.5%和90.9%。疫苗接种疫苗耐受性良好,22-39%的参与者报告出现局部反应,未观察到严重不良事件。然而,由于样本量较小且缺乏对照组,研究结果仍属初步,尚不足以改变HSCT受者的标准疫苗接种疫苗接种方案。结果提示,对于部分移植后青少年,两剂方案可能足够,但仍需进一步研究以确认此结论并评估对所有HPV型别的应答。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2026年8月27日发表。研究事件日期未明确说明,但研究对象为已接受异基因造血干细胞移植且术后至少一年的青少年。
地点与对象
该研究在泰国进行,重点关注接受过造血干细胞移植且移植后至少一年且已停止免疫抑制药物至少六个月的 9 至 15 岁青少年。
事件
泰国的一项小型试点研究检查了接受造血干细胞移植的青少年的抗体耐久性和 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 疫苗 的抗体耐久性。研究发现,两剂四价 HPV 疫苗 产生针对 HPV16 和 HPV18 的强烈而持久的抗体反应。
规模、影响与风险
这项研究涉及 23 名青少年,结果显示,在第一次接种疫苗六个月后,54.5% 的青少年已实现针对 HPV16 的血清转化,68.2% 的青少年已实现针对 HPV18 的血清转化。第二次注射后,95.5% 的人检测到针对 HPV16 的抗体,90.9% 的人检测到针对 HPV18 的抗体。研究还指出,疫苗接种 的耐受性良好,22-39% 的参与者报告出现局部反应,并且没有观察到严重的不良事件。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是一项试点调查,样本数量较少,并且没有未接种疫苗的对照组。它还没有理由取代所有 HSCT 接受者的现有建议。这些发现是初步的,需要通过更大的 队列研究 和更全面的评估进行进一步研究。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Study examines HPV antibody durability and immune response after stem cell transplantation - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A pilot study conducted in Thailand investigated the antibody response and immune durability of the human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine in adolescents who had undergone hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT). The research found that two doses of the quadrivalent HPV vaccine, targeting HPV16 and HPV18, produced strong and persistent antibody responses in most participants. Six months after the first dose, 54.5% had seroconverted against HPV16 and 68.2% against HPV18. After the second dose, these rates increased to 95.5% and 90.9%, respectively. The vaccination was well tolerated, with local reactions reported by 22-39% of participants and no serious adverse events observed. However, the study's small sample size and lack of a control group mean the findings are preliminary and do not yet justify changing standard vaccination schedules for HSCT recipients. The results suggest that a two-dose schedule may be sufficient for some adolescents post-transplantation, but further research is needed to confirm this and assess responses to all HPV types.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, August 29, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Study examines HPV antibody durability and immune response after stem cell transplantation by Bioengineer August 27, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A small pilot study from Thailand suggests that adolescents who undergo hematopoietic stem cell transplantation may develop a strong and persistent antibody response after receiving two doses of the human papillomavirus vaccine, challenging the assumption that three doses are always necessary for this medically vulnerable group. The findings, published in BMC Infectious Diseases, offer early evidence that a two-dose schedule can produce detectable antibodies against HPV types 16 and 18 in most young transplant recipients, while also maintaining measurable protection for years after vaccination. The results could eventually help doctors refine vaccination schedules for children and teenagers whose immune systems have been rebuilt after intensive cancer or blood-disorder treatment. Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation, or HSCT, is used to replace diseased or damaged blood-forming cells with healthy stem cells. Before the transplant, patients typically receive chemotherapy, total-body irradiation, or both to destroy malignant cells and suppress the immune system so that the donated cells can take hold. This conditioning process also eliminates many of the immune cells and antibody-producing memory cells generated by previous infections or vaccinations. As a result, transplant recipients are often treated as though they have lost much of their earlier vaccine protection and must be revaccinated according to specialized schedules. That issue is particularly important for HPV, a group of viruses transmitted mainly through sexual contact and responsible for nearly all cases of cervical cancer as well as a substantial proportion of anal, penile, vulvar, vaginal, and oropharyngeal cancers. HPV vaccines work best when administered before exposure to the virus, which is why routine vaccination focuses on adolescents. In healthy young people, the World Health Organization now recommends a single dose in many settings. Immunocompromised individuals, however, are generally advised to receive three doses because their immune systems may produce a weaker or less durable response. Whether adolescents recovering from HSCT truly need that full three-dose schedule has remained uncertain. Discover more Health Conditions Biological Sciences Health Foundations & Medical Research Cancer Infectious Diseases The research team at Ramathibodi Hospital and Mahidol University in Bangkok investigated the question through two complementary cohorts. The first included 23 adolescents between 9 and 15 years old who were at least one year beyond transplantation and had stopped taking immunosuppressive medicines for at least six months. Each participant received a quadrivalent HPV vaccine, which targets HPV types 6, 11, 16, and 18, at the start of the study and again six months later. Blood samples were collected six months after the first dose and one month after the second dose to determine whether the participants had developed antibodies against HPV16 and HPV18, two high-risk types strongly associated with cancer. The scientists measured antibodies directed against the L1 capsid proteins of HPV16 and HPV18. L1 is the major structural protein forming the outer shell of the virus, and vaccine particles made from virus-like assemblies of L1 train the immune system to recognize the pathogen without containing viral genetic material. The investigators used an enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay, or ELISA, to detect immunoglobulin G antibodies in blood serum. In practical terms, seroconversion meant that a participant’s antibody index crossed the study’s predefined threshold for a positive result, indicating that vaccination had generated a measurable immune response. Six months after the first dose, the response was incomplete but substantial: 54.5 percent of participants had seroconverted against HPV16, while 68.2 percent had seroconverted against HPV18. The second injection produced a striking increase. One month after the booster, 95.5 percent of adolescents had detectable antibodies against HPV16 and 90.9 percent had detectable antibodies against HPV18. The pattern suggests that the first dose primed the immune system in many participants, while the second dose activated immunological memory and generated a much broader antibody response. This boost is biologically important because antibodies can bind viral particles before they infect epithelial cells, blocking the earliest step in HPV transmission. The vaccination was also well tolerated. Local reactions, such as pain, redness, or swelling at the injection site, were reported by between 22 and 39 percent of participants during the first 48 hours. Systemic reactions were uncommon, and the researchers observed no serious adverse events. For transplant recipients, who may face complications including graft-versus-host disease, recurrent disease, and infections, the absence of serious vaccine-related problems is reassuring. Nevertheless, the safety findings must be interpreted cautiously because the cohort contained only 23 participants and was not large enough to detect rare complications. The study’s second cohort examined whether antibodies remained detectable over time. Fifteen vaccinated adolescents were assessed at intervals ranging from six months to 62 months after their final HPV vaccine dose. Every participant had detectable antibodies against HPV16, and all but one had detectable antibodies against HPV18. The cross-sectional design means that the researchers did not repeatedly follow the same individuals from vaccination onward, so the data cannot establish precisely how antibody concentrations changed in each participant. Even so, the results provide an encouraging snapshot of immune durability extending for more than five years in some adolescents. The authors emphasize that the findings do not yet justify replacing existing recommendations for all HSCT recipients. The study was a pilot investigation with small numbers, no unvaccinated comparison group, and antibody measurements focused on only two HPV types. Detectable antibodies are an important marker of protection, but the relationship between a laboratory antibody index and real-world prevention of HPV infection is not perfectly defined. The participants were also vaccinated after recovering sufficiently from transplantation, and the results may not apply to patients who remain on immunosuppressive therapy or who experience severe immune complications. Larger studies should track antibody titers serially, assess responses to all vaccine-covered HPV types, and compare two-dose and three-dose schedules directly. Despite those limitations, the study addresses a growing clinical challenge: how to restore vaccine protection efficiently without subjecting transplant survivors to unnecessary injections. Its results indicate that a two-dose quadrivalent schedule may be capable of producing near-universal antibody responses against HPV16 and HPV18 in selected adolescents once immune recovery has occurred. The evidence is not a final answer, but it supplies a valuable clue for future trials and could help guide individualized vaccination strategies. For young people who have already endured intensive treatment to rebuild their blood and immune systems, preserving protection against a preventable family of cancer-causing viruses may become an increasingly important part of long-term survivorship care. B routine priorityPublic-health news

What is HPV? Common myths about the virus linked to cervical cancer - The Independent

News Brief
This article from The Independent provides an overview of human papillomavirus (HPV), emphasizing its widespread nature and its association with various cancers, including cervical, anal, and oropharyngeal cancers. It highlights that HPV is one of the most diverse viral families, with over 200 types identified, some of which are low risk and cause benign warts, while others, such as HPV 16 and 18, are high risk and linked to cancer. The article notes that the HPV vaccine is recommended for both sexes to prevent infection, but it does not cover all cancer-causing strains or treat existing infections. It also mentions that only about 48% of girls worldwide are fully vaccinated, and public health efforts are ongoing to improve vaccination rates. The piece underscores the importance of condoms, hygiene practices, and regular cervical screening, even for vaccinated individuals, to mitigate the risk of HPV transmission and related cancers.

Thank you for registering Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in Jump to content Open full menu US Edition UK EditionEdición en Español Log in Log in News Sports Culture Lifestyle Money Travel Premium Climate More Swipe for next article Dismiss Lifestyle Health & Families What is HPV? Common myths about the virus linked to cervical cancer Scientists have identified more than 200 types of human papillomavirus, some of which have known links to cancer Copy link Bookmark Comments Go to comments Bookmark popover Removed from bookmarks Close popover Close Powered By 10 Sec 2.6K 00 No cervical cancer cases in young women fully vaccinated with HPV in Scotland Next video in Share Next KEEP WATCHING No cervical cancer cases in young women fully vaccinated with HPV in Scotland This article was originally published by The Conversation in June 2025, and is being republished by the Independent after it was announced that women in England will receive home cervical screening kits for HPV. Few viruses are as widespread – and sometimes misunderstood – as the human papillomavirus, or HPV. It’s so common that most of us – up to 80% – will encounter it at some point in our lives, often without even realising it. Understanding HPV matters, given that it is linked to several types of cancer. Scientists have identified more than 200 types of HPV, making it one of the most diverse viral families known – and a complex one at that. Many strains are low risk, causing either no symptoms or benign warts. HPV types 1, 2 and 4, for instance, are responsible for the common skin wart. Many will have experienced these, including the familiar verruca (plantar wart) picked up at swimming pools. Some strains, such as HPV 6 and 11, cause genital warts – small growths that appear on the genitals or around the anus. Treatments such as creams, surgical removal or freezing can get rid of the visible warts, but they don’t remove the virus itself. This means the virus can still be passed to sexual partners until the body’s immune system clears it. open image in gallery HPV is a DNA virus from the papillomavirus family (Alamy/PA) Most seriously, certain types of HPV – particularly 16 and 18 – have known links to cancer. They belong to a group of about 14 high-risk strains that can enter human cells and damage their DNA. This damage interferes with the cells’ normal controls on growth and division, which can lead to the development of cancer. RECOMMENDED Canada’s Carney responds to Trump’s Lake America name change with a history lesson Dolly Parton’s sister shares insight into country icon’s final months Ask a Pro: "How Long Does $2.5 Million Last in Retirement?"SmartAsset | SponsoredSponsored You'll Never Guess What Melts Tartar Off From Your Dog's Teeth (Not a Toothbrush)Petsnewsdaily | SponsoredSponsored Repeated or persistent infection with these strains increases the risk of developing cancer. So, too, does smoking, which reduces the ability of the immune system to clear the virus. Because HPV comes in so many forms – from harmless skin warts to strains linked with cancer – it’s easy to see how myths and confusion can take hold. To separate fact from fiction, here are five key points that everyone should know about the virus. 1. HPV is not just associated with cervical cancer While cervical cancer remains the most recognised HPV-related malignancy, the virus is also linked to cancers of the vulva, vagina, anus, penis, mouth and throat. Emerging evidence suggests some types may also contribute to developing skin cancer. This broad cancer risk explains why the widely available HPV vaccine is recommended for both sexes. The vaccine’s ability to prevent HPV infection makes population-wide immunisation beneficial, as transmission may occur between heterosexual and homosexual partners alike. 2. You don’t need to have symptoms or genital warts to pass the virus on HPV can remain on the skin for months before the immune system clears it, allowing transmission through contact even before genital warts appear and after they’ve been treated. This is why condoms should be used for at least three months after visible warts have resolved. 3. HPV transmission can occur from more than just vaginal or anal sex Oral and throat cancers can develop following HPV infection acquired through oral sex. The incidence of mouth and throat cancer is increasing worldwide, with oral sex now the most significant behavioural risk factor. Using condoms during oral sex can help reduce this risk. About the author Dan Baumgardt is a Senior Lecturer in the School of Psychology and Neuroscience at the University of Bristol. This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article. HPV can also spread through the use of sex toys. One study highlighted the ability of transmissible HPV to remain on sex toys and the need to develop proper hygiene practices for cleaning, and avoiding shared use. 4. Condoms are not 100% effective at preventing spread Condoms can lower the risk of HPV transmission, but they can’t offer full protection, as uncovered skin can still carry the virus. This is why many sexually active people will come into contact with a strain of the virus at some point in their lives, even when practising safe sex. 5. Even vaccinated women need to have smear tests Current HPV vaccines target the main high-risk virus types but cannot cover all cancer-causing strains, or treat existing infections. In rarer cases, cervical cancer can also arise without HPV infection. This is why women aged 25 to 64 are still invited for cervical screening every five years, even after vaccination. Women should also seek urgent medical review for other indicators of cervical cancer. These include pain or bleeding after sex, bleeding between periods or after menopause, and changes in vaginal discharge. open image in gallery A nurse injects a dose of human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine (AFP/Getty) Even though the HPV vaccine is widely available, uptake has dropped in some areas. The COVID pandemic disrupted routine vaccination programmes, while misinformation about the vaccine’s safety and effectiveness has shaken trust. In some places, low awareness of HPV’s link to different cancers – and of the need to vaccinate boys as well as girls – has also made public understanding more difficult. RECOMMENDED Emmerdale viewers left furious after fan favourite character dies Son of former Chelsea star dies hiking in 40C heat Cesar Millan Reveals What Honey Does to Your Aging DogPets Daily Health | SponsoredSponsored One Kitchen Ingredient Removes Tartar From Your Dog's Gum LinePetsnewsdaily | SponsoredSponsored The World Health Organisation has set a target of fully vaccinating 90% of girls by age 15 by 2030. At present, only about 48% of girls worldwide are fully vaccinated, so there is more work to be done. Although HPV is often harmless, the potential consequences of some strains are too significant to ignore. But no one should be fearful of an active sex life. For those eligible for the HPV vaccine, protection is not just for the individual, but also for future sexual partners who could otherwise be exposed. By staying informed and taking preventative measures, we can reduce the effects of this common virus and keep ourselves and others safer. 宫颈癌 筛查套件将发送给 NHS 中的数百万女性

Cervical cancer screening kits to be sent to millions of women on NHS
新闻简报英格兰国家医疗服务体系(NHS)宣布了一项新举措,向数百万未参加宫颈癌筛查预约的女性邮寄居家宫颈癌筛查试剂盒。该政策旨在通过提供一种隐私且便捷的替代方案,提高传统诊所检测的参与度。试剂盒将收集样本用于高危型HPV(人乳头瘤病毒)检测,已知该病毒可导致99.7%的宫颈癌病例。此次推广是NHS为在2040年前消除宫颈癌这一目标的一部分,目标人群为24至64岁女性。据最新数据显示,符合条件的女性中仅有68.8%及时完成筛查,低于80%的目标值。该举措获得卫生官员及倡导者支持,他们强调其在早期检测与预防中的作用。该项目预计将提升可及性并鼓励更多女性参与筛查,但无法取代临床医生检测。该政策于2026年8月25日宣布,将分阶段推广实施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该公告于 2026 年 8 月 25 日发布,从今天(2026 年 8 月 25 日)开始推出。
地点与对象
该倡议正在英格兰实施,目标对象是年龄在 24 岁至 64 岁之间、未参加子宫颈筛查预约的女性。
事件
英国 NHS 宣布向数百万未参加筛查预约的女性分发家用 宫颈癌 筛查套件。这是一项已确认的政策变化,旨在增加 宫颈癌 筛查的参与度。
规模、影响与风险
据估计,大约有 400 万女性未能及时进行筛查,该倡议旨在弥补这一差距。该风险并未明确说明,但该政策旨在通过早期检测高风险 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 来预防 宫颈癌。
应对、状态与不确定性
NHS 正在分阶段推出筛查套件,该举措是到 2040 年消除 宫颈癌 的更广泛战略的一部分。响应已得到确认并正在进行中,没有报告未解决的信息。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Cervical cancer screening kits to be sent to millions of women on NHS

News Brief
The NHS in England has announced a new initiative to send at-home cervical cancer screening kits to millions of women who have not attended screening appointments. This policy aims to increase participation by offering a discreet and convenient alternative to traditional clinic-based testing. The kits will collect samples for high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV) testing, which is known to cause 99.7% of cervical cancers. The rollout is part of the NHS's goal to eliminate cervical cancer by 2040, targeting women aged 24 to 64. According to the latest figures, only 68.8% of eligible women are up to date with screening, below the 80% target. The initiative is supported by health officials and campaigners, who emphasize its role in early detection and prevention. The program is expected to improve accessibility and encourage more women to take part in screening, though it is not a replacement for clinician-based tests. The policy was announced on 25 August 2026 and will be rolled out in phases.
Original source text (English)

MIRROR+ Mirror+ Exclusives Mirror+ Rewards Mirror+ Newsletters Celebs TV Emmerdale Coronation Street Films Hollywood Pride of Britain I'm A Celebrity Partner Stories Horoscopes Online Games Crossword Solitaire Sudoku Mahjong Bingo Sport Football Boxing UFC Cricket Rugby Union Rugby League F1 Racing Golf Athletics Darts Snooker US Sports Betting Lifestyle Family Health Sex & Relationships Money Gardening Shopping Fashion & Beauty Food & Drink Motoring Tech Gaming Travel UK & Ireland Europe USA & Canada Caribbean Africa Cruises Cheap Flights Asia & Middle East Australia & New Zealand Central & South America InYourArea Funeral Notices Shop Competitions Offers Newsletter Signup Follow us on social News Health Cervical cancer Cervical cancer screening kits to be sent to millions of women on NHS A radical shake-up will see tests mailed out to women in England who have not attended screening appointments so they can self-swab for cancer-causing high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV) News Dan Warburton Senior News Reporter 00:05, 25 Aug 2026Updated 00:07, 25 Aug 2026 What is cervical cancer? Millions of women will be sent at-home cervical cancer screening kits for free on the NHS, it was announced today. ‌ Campaigners welcomed the move which will see tests mailed out to those who have not attended screening appointments. Swabs used to collect a sample from the vagina will then posted back to NHS labs for free, where it will be tested for high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV). ‌ HPV is a group of viruses which can be passed on through sexual contact and cause no symptoms. However, around 13 high-risk types of the virus are known to cause 99.7% of cervical cancers. ‌ READ MORE: BBC Race Across The World star dies a year after her son and co-star Sam died in car crash READ MORE: 'I thought I had food poisoning from Egypt holiday but I was seriously ill' View 2 Images The tests will help identify high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV) which can cause 99.7% of cervical cancers(Image: Getty Images) Dr Sue Mann, national clinical director for women's health at NHS England, said: "Screening saves thousands of lives each year by preventing cancers and catching them earlier, but only around two thirds of women are attending cervical screening appointments when invited by the NHS. "Offering millions of women an alternative test they can do from the comfort of their own homes will be a game changer - making it easier than ever for them to get tested for HPV." ‌ Gem Sofianos-Boorman was diagnosed with cervical cancer at 28 after taking up her cervical screening invite. She said "it was the last thing" she expected, as she was "young, healthy, and had no symptoms". Ms Sofianos-Boorman, now 39, said: "Looking back, I truly believe that going to my screening appointment saved my life. My cancer was found early, which allowed me to have treatment before it spread. "That's why it's great news that the NHS is making it more accessible for some people to take part in screening. Cervical cancer often doesn't cause symptoms in its early stages, so screening plays a really important role in finding something before it might become serious." ‌ View 2 Images Dr Sue Mann said "screening saves thousands of lives each year by preventing cancers and catching them earlier" Eligible women aged between 30 and 65, who have not attended screening appointments, will be sent invitations from today through the NHS app, text message, email or by letter. They will then be able to order a kit through the NHS app or online. The phased rollout forms part of the NHS's target of eliminating cervical cancer by 2040. Women aged between 24 and 64 are invited for cervical screening every five years depending on their age, or more often if HPV is detected. Latest figures from NHS England show 68.8% of eligible women are up to date with cervical screening, below the 80% target. ‌ The health service estimates this means around four million women are not up to date. Dr Mann said: "There are lots of reasons that may stop women from getting their cervical screening - embarrassment, lack of time, or worries about discomfort - and these kits provide a discreet and convenient option we hope will encourage more women to take up the life-saving test. "So, if you're invited to take part, please order a test - it's a simple swab you can do at home in minutes." Athena Lamnisos, chief executive at The Eve Appeal, said: "Cervical screening is designed to prevent cervical cancer rather than respond to symptoms, attending a clinic requires people to actively prioritise an appointment among the many competing demands of everyday life. ‌ "Self-testing which can be done at home will be a step-change for many. We have the incredible opportunity to see the back of cervical cancer. How brilliant would that be? But to achieve this, we need to support everyone eligible to attend screening. "This is not a replacement for clinician screening, and we will continue to work with and support those attending their traditional cervical screening, to help make their appointments more comfortable and accessible." Health Secretary Yvette Cooper said: "Cervical cancer is preventable - but that's why we want to make it as easy and convenient as possible for women to keep up with their screening. These free self-testing kits will be offered to four million women who are currently behind on their screening to make it easier for as many women as possible to keep themselves healthy and safe. Article continues below "This is the future NHS we are building - there for all of us when we need it, and acting as early as possible to stop preventable deaths." Michelle Mitchell, chief executive of Cancer Research UK, said: "At-home HPV self-testing kits will help more people take part in a way that works for them. If people receive their cervical screening or self-testing kit invitation, please consider taking up the offer and encourage loved ones to do the same. "Beating cervical cancer means beating it for everyone, and this move brings us closer to that goal." ENJOYED THIS STORY? Event
The NHS in England announced the distribution of at-home cervical cancer screening kits to millions of women who have not attended screening appointments. This is a confirmed policy change aimed at increasing participation in cervical cancer screening.
Scale, impact and risk
Approximately four million women are estimated to be behind on their screening, and the initiative aims to address this gap. The risk is not explicitly stated, but the policy is designed to prevent cervical cancer by early detection of high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV).
Response, status and uncertainty
The NHS is implementing a phased rollout of the screening kits, and the initiative is part of a broader strategy to eliminate cervical cancer by 2040. The response is confirmed and ongoing, with no unresolved information reported.

研究进一步证实HPV疫苗对男性有益 - CIDRAP

Studies provide more evidence that HPV vaccines benefit men - CIDRAP
新闻简报CIDRAP于2026年8月27日报道,三项新研究表明,人乳头瘤病毒(HPV)疫苗显著降低男性感染风险,从而可预防头颈部癌症、肛门癌和阴茎癌。研究结果强调HPV疫苗在预防HPV相关疾病方面的广泛益处,超出其传统预防宫颈癌的作用。这些研究作为CIDRAP持续推广循证疫苗接种信息的一部分发表。报道未具体说明HPV在男性中的病例数或死亡数,但强调了HPV疫苗的保护效果。分析基于研究发现,未包含任何与HPV男性感染相关的确诊疫情或公共卫生紧急事件。研究表明HPV疫苗可能为男性提供显著健康益处,有助于预防多种HPV相关疾病。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Studies provide more evidence that HPV vaccines benefit men - CIDRAP

News Brief
CIDRAP reported on August 27, 2026, that three new studies demonstrate that HPV vaccines significantly reduce the risk of infection in men, which can lead to cancers of the head and neck, anus, and penis. The findings highlight the broader benefits of HPV vaccination beyond its traditional role in preventing cervical cancer. These studies were published as part of CIDRAP's ongoing efforts to promote evidence-based vaccine information. The report does not specify the number of cases or deaths associated with HPV in men but emphasizes the protective effect of vaccination. The analysis is based on research findings and does not include any confirmed outbreak or public health emergency related to HPV in men. The studies suggest that HPV vaccines may offer substantial health benefits to males, contributing to the prevention of various HPV-associated diseases.

随着家庭测试的推出,女性需要了解 宫颈癌 的哪些信息 - PA Media

What women need to know about cervical cancer – as at-home tests rolled out - PA Media
新闻简报英国 NHS 启动了一项计划,为未参加宫颈筛查预约的女性提供免费的家用 HPV 检测套件。该倡议于 2026 年 8 月 25 日开始,旨在通过允许女性自行收集样本并将其送往 NHS 实验室进行检测来增加对 宫颈癌 筛查的参与。如果检测到 高危型HPV,将邀请患者接受医疗保健专业人员的进一步评估。该计划强调了 HPV 在 宫颈癌 发展中的重要性,并指出高危类型(例如 HPV 16 和 18)的持续感染可能导致癌症。NHS 还强调,宫颈筛查可以在癌症形成之前检测细胞变化,并且 治疗 选项根据癌症的阶段和类型而有所不同。此次推出是改善 HPV 相关 宫颈癌 早期检测和预防的更广泛努力的一部分。
查看新闻五要素
时间
据文章报道,家用 HPV 检测试剂盒于 2026 年 8 月 25 日星期二开始推出。发布日期为2026年8月25日。
地点与对象
该计划正在英格兰推出,针对未参加宫颈筛查预约的女性。根据 NHS 指南,受影响人群包括 25 岁至 64 岁的女性。
事件
NHS 宣布为英格兰未参加宫颈筛查预约的女性推出免费的家用 HPV 检测套件。这是根据官方公告确认的事件。
规模、影响与风险
该倡议旨在通过提供家庭检测来增加宫颈筛查的参与度。它不报告具体病例数、死亡人数或暴露人数。该风险被明确指出与持续的 高危型HPV 感染有关,这可能导致 宫颈癌。
应对、状态与不确定性
NHS 正在积极推出检测套件,并邀请患有 高危型HPV 的女性参加进一步的筛查预约。该举措是旨在改善 宫颈癌 预防和检测的更广泛公共卫生努力的一部分。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

What women need to know about cervical cancer – as at-home tests rolled out - PA Media

News Brief
The NHS in England has launched a program offering free at-home HPV testing kits to women who have not attended their cervical screening appointments. This initiative, which began on August 25, 2026, aims to increase participation in cervical cancer screening by allowing women to collect samples themselves and send them to NHS laboratories for testing. If high-risk HPV is detected, patients are invited for further assessment with healthcare professionals. The program highlights the importance of HPV in cervical cancer development, noting that persistent infections with high-risk types, such as HPV 16 and 18, can lead to cancer. The NHS also emphasizes that cervical screening can detect cellular changes before cancer forms, and that treatment options vary depending on the stage and type of cancer. The rollout is part of a broader effort to improve early detection and prevention of HPV-associated cervical cancer.

Photography and video to connect brands and audiences PA Meets Virtual events for the PR & comms sector Briefings Keeping you abreast of industry news that matters What women need to know about cervical cancer – as at-home tests rolled out By Camilla Foster, PA 25/08/2026 The NHS has announced that women across England who have not attended their cervical screening appointments will be offered free at-home HPV testing kits. The rollout for these new self-testing kits began on Tuesday, August 25, in a bid to encourage more women to take part in screening for human papillomavirus (HPV), a virus that can increase the risk of cervical cancer. The kits include a vaginal swab, which allows women to collect a sample themselves at home. The sample can then be posted free of charge to an NHS laboratory, where it will be tested for high-risk HPV. If high-risk HPV is detected, the patient will be invited to attend a cervical screening appointment with a healthcare professional for further assessment. Following the announcement, we spoke to Dr Lucy Lord, founding partner and women’s health lead at Central Health London, who shared five important things every woman should know about cervical cancer... 1) HPV is very common, but only some types are high risk The human papillomavirus (HPV) causes almost all of the main types of cervical cancer (Alamy/PA) There are many different types of HPV and although most are harmless, some can cause changes that can develop into cancer. “HPV is a very common virus. If you are sexually active, you will have almost certainly caught at least one of the types of HPV viruses,” says Lord. Most people’s immune system clears about half of HPV infections within six to 12 months, but if you have a long-lasting (persistent) infection with a high-risk type of HPV you are more at risk of developing cervical cancer, according to Cancer Research UK’s website. Around 14 types of HPV are considered high risk for cervical cancer. Two of these types (HPV 16 and HPV 18) cause about 70 out of 100 (70%) of all cervical cancer cases, according to Cancer Research UK. “Some of them are oncogenic viruses, so are known to cause cancer,” says Lord. “They can insert themselves into the DNA of the vulnerable cells in the squamocolumnar junction [on the cervix], and cause mutations within those cells that turn them into cancer.” 2) The HPV vaccine offers effective protection against high-risk HPV strains and related cancers All children aged 12 to 13 (school year 8) are offered the HPV vaccine (Alamy/PA) The HPV vaccine helps protect against HPV and is recommended for children aged 12 to 13 years old and people at higher risk from HPV, according to the NHS website. “In private practice, we offer women in their 20s and 30s who missed being vaccinated a HPV vaccine,” says Lord. “Although it might not be cost-effective from a population perspective, it’s very effective for that individual, and it will still reduce the risk of them catching a virus that is around long enough to cause cancer.” 3) Irregular bleeding is a common symptom of cervical cancer “There are some indicators of cervical cancer. For example, irregular bleeding or bleeding in between your periods, and particularly bleeding after intercourse,” says Lord. “Abnormal discharge and blood stain discharge are also symptoms of cervical cancer.” Pain in your lower back, between your hip bones (pelvis), or in your lower tummy is also listed as another common symptom of cervical cancer on the NHS website. 4) Routine cervical screening can detect cellular changes before cancer forms Women are first invited for a cervical screening on the NHS from age 25 (Alamy/PA) “There are several stages (mild, moderate and severe) that can be detected before cancer develops,” says Lord. “A pap smear can spot abnormal or pre-malignant changes in the cells and there are several ways to treat the precancerous cells to stop them from becoming cancer.” All women aged 25 to 64 are invited for cervical screening every five years, according to the NHS website. 5) Cervical cancer can be treated The treatment for cervical cancer depends on the size and type of cervical cancer you have, where the cancer is, if it has spread and on the patient’s general health. For some very early cervical cancers, it might be possible to remove all of the cancer with a large loop excision of the transformation zone (LLETZ) or a cone biopsy, according to Cancer Research UK. “There are also different surgical procedures for cervical cancer, but if the cancer is too far advanced to be treated with a LLETZ biopsy or surgery, radiotherapy and chemotherapy are the standard treatments,” says Lord. View five news elements
Time
The rollout of at-home HPV testing kits began on Tuesday, August 25, 2026, as reported in the article. The publication date is August 25, 2026.
Location and population
The initiative is being rolled out in England, targeting women who have not attended their cervical screening appointments. The affected population includes women aged 25 to 64, as per NHS guidelines.
Event
The NHS announced the rollout of free at-home HPV testing kits for women in England who have not attended cervical screening appointments. This is a confirmed event based on the official announcement.
Scale, impact and risk
The initiative aims to increase participation in cervical screening by offering at-home testing. It does not report specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers. The risk is explicitly stated as related to persistent high-risk HPV infections, which can lead to cervical cancer.
Response, status and uncertainty
The NHS is actively rolling out the testing kits and inviting women with high-risk HPV to attend further screening appointments. The initiative is part of a broader public health effort to improve cervical cancer prevention and detection.

新分析显示,九岁至二十六岁之间的 HPV 疫苗接种 与喉癌大幅减少有关 - 医学日报

HPV Vaccination Between Ages Nine and Twenty Six Was Linked to Sharply Fewer Throat Cancers in New Analysis - Medical Daily
新闻简报《医学日报》发表的一项新分析表明,9 至 26 岁之间的 HPV 疫苗接种 与喉癌病例的显着减少有关。该研究强调了 疫苗 对与 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 相关的某些癌症的潜在保护作用。然而,该报告没有提供具体病例或死亡人数,也没有详细说明方法或进一步的公共卫生应对措施。该研究结果基于对 HPV 相关疾病及其预防的更广泛审查,强调了 疫苗接种 在降低癌症风险方面的重要性。尽管该研究提供了有希望的见解,但它仍然是一份初步报告,可能需要更多的研究来证实这些结果及其对公共卫生策略的影响。

规模、影响与风险

应对、状态与不确定性

B routine priorityPublic-health news

HPV Vaccination Between Ages Nine and Twenty Six Was Linked to Sharply Fewer Throat Cancers in New Analysis - Medical Daily

News Brief
A new analysis published by Medical Daily suggests that HPV vaccination between the ages of nine and twenty-six is associated with a significant reduction in throat cancer cases. The study highlights the potential protective effect of the vaccine against certain cancers linked to human papillomavirus (HPV). However, the report does not provide specific case or death counts, nor does it detail the methodology or further public health responses. The findings are based on a broader review of HPV-related diseases and their prevention, emphasizing the importance of vaccination in reducing cancer risk. While the study offers promising insights, it remains a preliminary report, and more research may be needed to confirm these results and their implications for public health strategies.
